ParlaMint-AT_2012-06-14-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00161_d8e3313	Mrs Bundesminister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-06-14-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00161_d8e3329	Ladies and gentlemen, Mr Jarolim! Of course, I assume that the Austrian implementation of the EU Directive on data storage, which has also been decided here in the National Council with the approval of your group, is in conformity with fundamental rights. Of course, I do not want to prejudge the decision of the Constitutional Court. On this occasion, however, I would also like to stress that we have only made minimal implementation of the EU Directive when it comes to data storage and, of course, have taken into account the high standards of Austrian data protection and fundamental rights. The legal design has already benefited from the experience of other Member States and, of course, also from national constitutional jurisdiction. Let me briefly refer to the explanations on the text of the law to support the just said statement. I quote: It is necessary to ensure that “the fundamental rights interventions associated with the storage of data are as low as possible – and therefore proportionately for the purpose pursued – that the security of the data is best ensured both by the telecommunications operators and by the authorities entitled to data application, which comply with the data protection requirements required by the data protection law, and that all necessary legal means are available for the pursuit of data protection and fundamental interests of the data subject” () – citations. As far as the European Commission's announcements concerning an amendment to the EU Directive are concerned, I can tell you that the Commission considers that the evaluation of impact assessment and data collection has not yet been completed, so that no opinion on possible amendments can yet be made by my side at this time. However, the European Commission is constantly stressing that the proposed amendment should not lead to some Member States not now implementing the existing EU Directive. In other words, the existing EU Directive must be implemented by the Member States, regardless of the fact that the Commission is working on possible amendments.

A working group has been set up in the Ministry to deal precisely with these questions: Where can we make the necessary changes in family law in order to create a better situation for children, for example in the case of parental separation? These are the situations in which the children suffer particularly, and of course you have to do so. In this working group the aim was to bring together the divergent interests of mothers, fathers and, in particular, children and to find a common solution. To this end, we have invited representatives of the Federal Ministries concerned, but of course also representatives of science, the case law and also the legal professions, clearly also interested representatives of mothers, fathers and children. It has really succeeded in finding common solutions, and now it is about making a government template out of it, or first of all – we are working on it – to draw up a draft assessment. We are working on the preparation of the text of the law and the explanations. In the next step – and I am confident that this will take place before the summer – there will be first political clarifications. If everything has been clarified at the political level, we can go into the review. I hope all of this goes fast, for the benefit of the children. This should also be at the heart of the matter, which is perfectly clear to me. However, I must also stress that the changes I have planned are not only about care – this is always a bit shortened on care – but also about improvements in the right to visit, I am concerned with improvements in family law procedures, procedural reductions, and I am also concerned with changes in name rights.

It should be possible for family judges to set up mandatory measures, such as the mandatory visit to a parent's advice, a family consultation or the mandatory visit to an initial discussion on mediation or conciliation procedures. In my opinion, these instruments will make it easier to move parents to focus more on the child, to put their own feelings, their own injuries in the background and perhaps to think more about what they can best do for the child. That is why I believe that the common care can succeed, even in those cases where there is no agreement at first on the common care, in order to still be effective and correct.

It is quite possible for me to propose a change in civil law, i.e. an extension during the limitation periods. The focus was always on time limits for criminal law, but I think we should also look at the time limits for civil law. For process support: We have had very good experience in criminal law with legal and psychosocial process support. This works really well, and it is very important for the victims that they have both legal and psychosocial support. To what extent this can be extended to civil law, we must look at it. [[President Mag. Prammer gives the bell sign.]] This is, of course, also a budgetary question for us, but one can see in detail in which civil law is so useful.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-06-14-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00161_d8e6040	Additional question? – Mr Glaser, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-06-14-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00161_d8e6058	Thank you, Mrs. Federal Minister! Even now, the limitation periods for sexual offences are relatively wide, and in the case of unlimited limitation periods, about 30 or 40 years, the likelihood of finding evidence is, as you have said yourself, relatively limited. My specific question: How do you assess the risk of admissibility for lack of evidence in the case of these unlimited limitation periods?
ParlaMint-AT_2012-06-14-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00161_d8e6076	Mrs Bundesminister, please.
ParlaMint-AT_2012-06-14-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00161_d8e6097	It is, of course, the fact that the evidence, the longer the act lies behind, more and more dilutes. The longer the period between action and investigation, the more difficult it will be to provide evidence and make the findings necessary for a debt claim. Then it may be that witnesses have already passed away, that documents are no longer visible or have been destroyed, that local circumstances have completely changed, and of course there are memory gaps with the victim and with the witnesses. All of this is, of course, much more difficult to identify through the course of time. In order not to frustrate here for many years, it is really better, from the point of view of the victims, to provide for a limitation period in order not to come to the secondary vigilance I have already mentioned. [[President Mag. Prammer gives the bell sign.]] It is really often suggested to the victims that there will be a culprit, and then they will experience an acquittal of lack of evidence: That's frustrating for the victims. Then the impression arises that they are not believed, and this also increases the mental burden, especially by the fact that much will go through the process again, which has been experienced. And if an acknowledgment is made, I really don't think that's reasonable.

I believe that this should also be emphasized, because one cannot overlook the fact that there is currently intensive negotiations between the Federal Government and the Federal Government, for example with regard to the administrative reform especially in the school administration, that there is currently negotiations between the Federal Government and the staff representation of teachers on a new, modern teacher service law, and that there are currently intensive scientific preparatory work on the new training of educators. This is simply to be seen – with all the clarity that we document in our proposals today – in the positions where we expressly define common positions – and on the other hand we must recognise and accept that such important negotiations, especially between employers and workers, must not be completely prejudicated. Those who feel prepared, however, can implement them at the originally planned time. I am thinking of the education standards, the new school supervision, the expansion of day care, the expansion of afternoon care and all day schools. We have a number of reforms in front of us, ladies and gentlemen, which were not least the subject of the debate with the representatives and experts of the educational people, such as the reorganisation of the 9. School level, the theme of middle maturity, i.e. to link the end of compulsory education not only to an age but rather to the achievement of an educational goal. I am thinking of the question of folk school, a reliable folk school in which the cultural techniques are taught in such a way that the children are also safe in reading, writing and computing. I am thinking of the further strengthening of school locations, a greater degree of autonomy and responsibility at the site and of course the topics I have already mentioned, the new teacher training, the service law and the reform in the administration. We have not made the debate on the motions for resolutions easy, and I would also like to address this explicitly, because sometimes it is said that resolutions would be like a first-class funeral. I would like to say clearly that this also depends, of course, on the content of such a motion for a resolution and whether it has, for example, deadlines or not. In this context, I refer to the resolution on administrative reform, where we have very specific points, where we also have a clear time until we ask the Federal Government to present us with legislative proposals. I am thinking of the very concrete statements relating to elementary education or, for example, the resolution on the continuation of the educational reform process in the tertiary education sector or on day care. Overall, I would like to stress that the debate on the content of the educational people's desire was a very good discussion, was a very constructive debate and is a debate that will not be in the sand, but rather a debate that encourages us to continue in the educational reform. It is not true that there has been no educational reforms before, nor is it true that after the debate on education people's desire there will be no further educational reforms. Of course, both are the case, and I think we are on a very, very good path. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2012-06-14-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00161_d8e10731	Mr President. Dear Mrs. Dr. Schmied! Ladies and gentlemen! Dear initiators of popular desire! My Dear Ones Ladies and gentlemen on the courts! I too am very grateful to the initiators of popular desire that they have placed the educational topic so prominently. That was a very, very important step. I can also express my solidarity with many of the demands of this popular desire – especially concerning the tertiary sector, some demands are also made. I agree to this first and allow myself to join a few general considerations on the tenor of popular desire. In the tertiary sector, the desire – once again, one can say – is to spend 2% of the gross domestic product on the tertiary sector by 2020. This is a very demanding goal, but it is a goal which has been expressed many times as a goal in this House and to which I stand. We are still on our way to this goal by providing a billion higher education for the next three years. Let's compare this with other countries: It was just at the beginning of this week a two-day meeting of the European Higher Education Association in Salzburg, and it has been seen that in the meantime very, very many countries are also radically shortening in the tertiary area. We are a glorious exception here. Indeed, we take the education sector out of and invest in our other consolidation efforts. This has to be stressed very clearly and clearly here. It must also be said, however, that these university billions are not sufficient for the 2-percent target alone. We urgently need more resources from the private sector. Austria spends above-average public resources on the tertiary sector, but it invests below-average funds from the private sector, and this private sector is simply a subject for study. This is indispensable: Study contributions are a major contribution to the financing of universities. Study contributions are socially fair if they are suspended by aid systems. They are socially fair, above all, because we have very, very many students from abroad – they are very welcome to us, universities live from internationality – but it is not to be seen that the Austrian taxpayer comes up to the full cost of studying foreign students who are sometimes very high, namely up to €40 000 a year. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] And study contributions also give time to an effect that is also addressed in popular desire, namely an increase in the graduate rate. Study contributions increase the liability of study. You study faster, you study more purposefully. [[Abg. Ing. Hofer: Does this also apply to the Federal Chancellor?]] So they have all around positive effects and are essentially part of the funding of the tertiary sector. Among the technical universities whose expansion – a doubling by 2017 – is also required: There too we ended a long stagnation. We are now giving the technical colleges more money for more places to study. A total of 4,000 will be in the coming years, i.e. an increase of more than 10 percent. The doubling by 2017, which is required, we will not achieve, but we are on a good path. Finally, the popular desire also calls for the funding of a study place. Here too we are in good negotiations with the coalition partner. We hope that we will be able to present a result in the near future. It is clear, and this is also important to me, that this funding will, of course, not be able to finance the places at universities unlimitedly – that is simply not possible; we cannot finance unlimited places for Europe as a whole – that is, the funding of a study place also implies an access system which is also useful and correct for other reasons. We have agreed that this access regime should in no way lead to a reduction in the number of places of study, but it must ensure that universities can live their capacity, which they always have. It can't be that a university is forced to take in five times as many students as she can. Everything's suffering. This is also a fraud to the young people who think they have good conditions – but they do not find them. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] This leads me to another point of desire, the academic ratio, where an increase of 40% is required. This is plausible in view of the international situation, it is less plausible to consider the special training courses in Austria. If we count on what has already been educated in other countries, we already have a quota of about 35 percent. We have a higher education admission rate of 57 percent. Germany and Switzerland, the currently probably most economically successful countries in Europe, have access rates of about 40 percent. In other words, for the economy, a high degree of academicism is not necessarily desirable. On the contrary, the European Parliament has recently set Austria as a special example of dual training. On the contrary, we must strengthen them. This is a special strength in Austria, as I have always been confirmed by international economic representatives. [[Congratulations on the ÖVP.]] And on this occasion I would also like to say that in this context the popular desire and also some speeches imply a tenor that I do not like. It's always talking about deadlocks, about educational deadlocks. I am also against every dead end, but if you look more closely, then the main school is very often meant by the dead end, or then perhaps later by the New Secondary School. I hope not, but if that is meant by that, then the choice of words is completely missing. A primary school is neither institutionally a dead end nor what their professional prospects are. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and FPÖ.]] It is not institutionally because in many federal countries more students of the primary school come to Matura as students of the general higher schools. She's not a dead end! And above all, it is not a dead end because not every successful life has to pass through a university. There is excellent vocational training. There are excellent professions in Austria that do much for the Austrian economy, which is not university, and therefore one may call paths that do not lead to the university, but in other vocational trainings, never and never as a dead end. [[Congratulations with ÖVP and FPÖ.]] Thankfully, the popular desire also addresses the training of educators. This is a very, very important field, especially when considering a demand of popular desire which it is already addressing in its first point. A largely autonomous school system stands and falls with a high quality of good teacher education, and of course this must start with early childhood education, with the kindergartens and kindergartens. The universities are in the process of increasingly introducing teaching chairs, and these chairs will lead us to more academic training in this field. And I hope that the institutions, who then make appropriate appointments, will benefit from this opportunity to ask for high quality here. [[President Neugebauer gives the bell sign.]] Overall, I would like to ask, with regard to all the rights of improvement and reform, which we have and which we see: We must not make the Austrian education and university system that bad! It's much better than we do! The universities are not desolat, as unfortunately one can also hear from the initiators of popular desire, the schools are better than it shows PISA. We also need to see our strengths. And that's why my final appeal is: We must see our strengths and not weaken our strengths, but we must strengthen our weaknesses. Thank you.

That's once the state laws. But now I need to know in addition what regulations each municipality has made. We have received the opinion, and I must say, ladies and gentlemen, what has come back from the national conference on the subject is, in my view, a naughtiness. A uniform regulation on the behaviour of dogs was nothing other than: Throughout Austria, dogs are to be led on the leash, and when released, they have a muzzle to wear, except on free run zones, areas that determine the communities. If something is quite simple, you can easily implement it. The national conference will write back the following: "On the basis of suggestions from animal welfare organisations and dog associations, the national conference () advised whether by way of a kind. 15a B-VG agreement at least the essential provisions of dog husbandry could be regulated at national level. Exploring the positions of the individual countries on this issue showed that harmonisations are difficult for a variety of reasons and therefore the willingness of a corresponding kind. 15a B-VG Agreement does not exist.’; That is to say, nine heads of state or eight heads of state and one head of state see themselves unable to determine that in Austria all dogs are to be led on the leash and then, if they walk freely, they have a muzzle to wear. If this is difficult for our duke and duke, ladies and gentlemen, then I can only say one thing: Let us resolve these countries, because then we certainly don't need them anymore.

ParlaMint-AT_2012-06-14-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00161_d8e17555	Madam President. Mr President of the Court of Auditors! (Parliament adopted the legislative resolution) Mr Lapp has already explained what chapters have been examined. From my point of view, a very important chapter, which also contributes to saving in this country, is military representation abroad. There has been a review of the military attachés in various areas: first of all in human resources, second in the field of property management and third in the field of responsibilities and competences between ministries. This Court of Auditors report clearly shows that there was no systematic and comprehensible overall analysis of benefits. In the field of personnel, it was planned to save 35 staff – so far only 19 posts have been saved. The Court of Auditors also pointed out that this resulted in €2.3 million of additional expenditure. The aim of reducing the number of defence attachés in EU countries by six has been implemented only half so far, and only three offices have been saved. The Court of Auditors has also recommended increasing the establishment of a travel attaché so that one can take care of these countries from Vienna, from home. The Court of Auditors also questioned critically the military representation in Brussels. It is about that we have used 24 staff in the area of the European Defence Agency and 26 people in international functions. The property management sector: We currently have 24 residences, nine are owned by the Republic and 15 properties have been rented. In this respect, the Court of Auditors has critically shown that there was no economic comparison between buying, renting and housing costs. However, this should be introduced in the future – and the Minister of State has promised to do so in the Committee – with all the properties and residences. The Court of Auditors has also clearly indicated that some of the properties have not been used, for example, the property in Ankara has been empty for three years, and the residence size has also been significantly exceeded there and there, with a requirement of approximately 300 square meters. The responsibilities: There are indeed savings potential and synergies between the Federal Ministry of State Defense and the Federal Ministry of European and International Affairs. There have, I believe, been no clear definitions in the field of law enforcement, in the field of supervision and supervision. And, of course, it was also critically shown that staff management was divided into seven organizational units. There are a total of 20 recommendations from the Court of Auditors to the Military Attachés, 14 of which have been implemented, and six are in implementation, according to Federal Minister Darabos. Mr Lapp mentioned earlier that eight Property Sales took place, with a profit of €5.5 million. I believe that it is important that this Court of Auditors report contributes to pooling the military requirements and tasks in the future, that we are here sparingly set up and operate in the sense of administrative reform, use synergies, and, quite importantly, I believe, that the introduction of quality management is also important. Thank you.

ParlaMint-AT_2012-06-14-024-XXIV-NRSITZ-00161_d8e20681	Mr President. Ladies and gentlemen! I would like to make a few fundamental points here, namely, that in recent years we have been able to observe two trends in the development of the Austrian Parliament and in the development of the European Union. Let us start with the European Union. We have a development that has been taking place for years and is as follows: gradually, successively, EU competence will be deprived of the individual national states, it goes towards an EU-superstate, towards an EU-unity state, towards a model of the United States of Europe, as is always quoted as we do not want. - That's the one trend. In the other direction, we have paralleled this development – and it will continue today with this first reading, which has already been criticised many times here – that we give up the sovereignty of Austria gradually, gradually, successively, silently and silently, without informing the population, and gradually abandon the parliamentary co-determination of this House and say goodbye to it. These two tendencies need to be critically questioned, these two tendencies need to be critically highlighted, and these two tendencies must ultimately also be addressed. We do not want an EU-superstate! [[BZÖ and FPÖ are welcome.]] We do not want a task from Austrian sovereignty to Brussels. We want a strong, independent parliament elected by the people. These are the basic principles pursued by BZÖ. These are also the basic principles – if you are now taking a historical look, ladies and gentlemen – that the founding fathers of this united Europe have pursued. These were also their basic principles after the Second World War, after this great disaster, under the motto “Never again war! ” We create a Federation, a common European Federation of Sovereign National States, which work together at European level, but remain sovereign and independent. That was the basic idea of the founding fathers of this European Union. I am now asking – and today, in 2012, we are all asking this question: What has become of this idea of the founding fathers?
